7. The Poincaré–Lelong formula and first Chern delta-currents
   The Poincaré–Lelong formula in complex analysis is of fundamental impor-
tance for Arakelov theory. Chambert–Loir and Ducros have shown in [CLD12,
§4.6] that the Poincaré–Lelong formula holds as an identity between currents
on Berkovich spaces while Theorem 7.2 below enhances the Poincaré–Lelong
formula as an equality of δ-currents. We use the Poincaré–Lelong formula to
define the first Chern δ-current of a continuously metrized line bundle.
58                    WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

7.1. Let X be a variety over K of dimension n and let f ∈ K(X) \ {0}. In
Example 6.11, we have introduced the δ-current of integration δX leading to
the definition of the δ-current δZ for any cycle Z on X. Using that for the Weil
divisor cyc(f ) of f , we get a δ-current δcyc(f ) on X an .
   On the other hand, the complement U of the support of the principal Cartier
divisor div(f ) is an open dense subset of X. By Proposition 6.5, we get the
δ-current [log |f |] ∈ E 0,0 (U an ) = E 0,0 (X an ).
Theorem 7.2. For a non-zero rational function f on X, the Poincaré–Lelong
equation                                           
                         δcyc(f ) = d0 d00 log |f |
holds in E 1,1 (X an ).
Proof. The proof is similar as in [CLD12, §4.6], but it is more on the tropical
side as we do not have integrals of δ-forms over analytic subdomains at hand.
We will first do some reduction steps and then we will introduce some notation
which allows us to use results from [CLD12]. The claim is local on X an and so
we may assume that X = Spec(A) and f ∈ A. The latter induces a morphism
f : X → A1 . We may assume that the morphism is not constant as otherwise
all terms are 0. Since A is a domain, the property S1 of Serre is satisfied.
   Let us recall some results from [CLD12] before we start the actual proof. Let
W be an affinoid subdomain of X an and let g : W → T an be an analytic map
for T = Grm . Following [CLD12], we call such a map to a torus an analytic
moment map. We obtain a continuous map
                           gtrop := trop ◦ g : W → Rr .
We get an analytic map h := (f, g) : W → (A1 )an × T an . We denote the
fibre of W over t ∈ (A1 )an by Wt with respect to the restriction of f to W .
If I is an interval in (0, ∞), then WI := |f |−1 (I) ∩ W . We observe that Wt
and WI carry natural structures of analytic spaces of dimension n − 1 and n
respectively. It follows from general results of Ducros [Duc12, Théorème 3.2]
that the sets gtrop (Wt ) and htrop (WI ) are integral R-affine polyhedral sets of
dimension less or equal to n − 1 and n respectively. These polyhedral sets can
be equipped with natural integral weights. A construction of these so called
tropical weights can be found in [Gub16, §7] or in [CLD12, 3.5] in the language
of calibrations. We observe that the tropical weights take the multiplicities of
irreducible components into account. The k-skeleton of a polyhedral set P of
dimension at most k is by definition the union of all k-dimensional polyhedra
contained in P . By [CLD12, Proposition 4.6.6], there exist a real number r > 0
and an integral R-affine polyhedral complex C in Rr of pure dimension n − 1
with integer weights m such that all polyhedra in C are polytopes with the
following properties:
    (a) for every t in the closed ball in (A1 )an with center 0 and radius r, the
        (n−1)-skeleton of gtrop (Wt ) endowed with the canonical tropical weights
        is equal to (C , m);
    (b) for every closed interval I ⊂ (0, r], the n-skeleton of htrop (WI ) endowed
        with the canonical analytic tropical weights is equal to (− log(I), 1) ×
        (C , m) as a product of weighted polyhedral complexes.
      A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                           59

In fact, Chambert–Loir and Ducros formulated this crucial result in terms of
canonical calibrations instead of analytic tropical weights. We refer to [Gub16,
§7] for the definition and translation of these equivalent notions. The analytic
space W0 coincides with the closed analytic subspace of W determined by the
effective Cartier divisor div (f |W ). Using (a) for t = 0, we see that (C , m)
is equal to the (n − 1)-skeleton of gtrop (div(f |W )) as a weighted polyhedral
complex.
   After recalling these preliminary results, we start with the proof. Since the
δ-currents δcyc(f ) and d0 d00 [log |f |] are symmetric, it is enough to check the
Poincaré–Lelong equation by evaluating at a symmetric α ∈ Bcn−1,n−1 (X an ).
The δ-form α is given by tropical charts (Vi , ϕUi )i∈I covering X an and symmetric
αi ∈ AZ n−1,n−1 (Vi , ϕUi ). Since α has compact support, there are finitely many
i such that the corresponding Vi ’s cover supp(α). In the following, we restrict
our attention to these finitely many i’s and we number them by i = 1, . . . , m.
   Let us consider the very affine open subset U := U1 ∩ · · · ∩ Um \ supp(div(f ))
of X. Let Gi : NU,R → NUi ,R (resp. F : NU,R → R) be the canonical affine map
compatible with tropU and tropUi (resp. − log |f |). Let x0 be the coordinate
on R and let Hi := (F, Gi ) : NU,R → R × NUi ,R .
   For every x ∈ supp(α), there is an i ∈ {1, . . . , m} such that x ∈ Vi . We choose
an integral Γ-affine polytope ∆i of maximal dimension in NUi ,R containing
tropUi (x) in its interior. We may assume that ∆i ∩ Trop(Ui ) ⊆ tropUi (Vi ).

Remark 7.4. Let f denote a regular function on the affine variety X. The proof
of Lemma 7.3 given above shows that equation (7.3.1) holds more generally for
any generalized δ-forms α on X an with compact support. If we permute the
roles of d0 and d00 , we obtain by the same argument that
                     Z
(7.4.1)         −                      d0 F ∗ (x0 ) ∧ αU = hδcyc(f ) , αi
                    ∂(tropU (W (s)∩U an ))

holds for all generalized δ-forms α ∈ Pcn−1,n−1 (X an ). An elegant way to deduce
(7.4.1) is to apply (7.3.1) for J ∗ (α) and to use symmetry of the δ-current of
integration.
7.5. Let ϕ denote an invertible analytic function on some open subset W of
X an . Given x ∈ W there exists by [Gub16, Proposition 7.2] an open subset U
of X, an algebraic moment map f : U → Gm and an open neighbourhood V of
x in U an ∩ W such that − log |ϕ| and − log |f | agree on V . It follows that the
function − log |ϕ| belongs to A0 (W ) and we get
(7.5.1)                 d0 d00 [− log |ϕ|] = −[d0 d00 log |ϕ|] = 0
from (6.9.1) and the trivial case of the Poincaré–Lelong formula where f is
invertible.
7.6. Let L be a line bundle on X and let W be an open subset of X an . We fix
an open covering (Ui )i∈I of X, a family (si )i∈I of nowhere vanishing sections
                                                        ×
si ∈ Γ(Ui , L), and the 1-cocyle (hij ) with values in OX determined by sj = hij si .
Recall that a continuous metric k k on L over W is given by a family (ρi )i∈I of
continuous functions ρi : Uian ∩W → R such that ρj = |hij |ρi on (Ui ∩Uj )an ∩W
for all i, j ∈ I. An analytic section s ∈ Γ(V, Lan ) on some open subset V of W
determines as follows a continuous function ksk : V → R. We write s = fi si
for some analytic function fi on V ∩ Uian and define ksk = |fi | · ρi on V ∩ Uian .
Observe that we have ρi = ksi k on Uian ∩ W .
7.7. Let L be a line bundle on X endowed with a continuous metric k k over
the open subset W of X an . Then we define the first Chern current associated to
the metrized line bundle (L|W , k k) as the δ-current [c1 (L|W , k k)] ∈ E 1,1 (W )
given locally on W ∩ U an by d0 d00 [− log ks|U an ∩W k] for any trivialization U
of L with nowhere vanishing section s ∈ Γ(U, L). Here, we have used that
a continuous function defines a δ-current as explained in Proposition 6.16.
Since d0 d00 [− log |ϕ|] = 0 for an invertible analytic function ϕ, the δ-current
[c1 (L|W , k k)] is well-defined on W and we may even use analytic trivializa-
tions in the definition. Obviously, the formation of the first Chern current is
compatible with tensor product of metrized line bundles as usual.
   If the metric is smooth then [c1 (L, k k)] is the current associated to the first
Chern form c1 (L|W , k k) defined in [CLD12]. In general, the notion c1 (L|W , k k)
has no meaning as a form and we use brackets to emphasize that [c1 (L|W , k k)]
is a δ-current. In Section 9, we will introduce metrics for which c1 (L|W , k k)
has a meaning as a δ-form.
Corollary 7.8. Let L be a line bundle on X endowed with a continuous metric
k k over the open subset W of X an . For every non-trivial meromorphic section
64                      WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

s of L with associated Weil divisor Y , the equality
                                                        
                   [c1 (L|W , k.k)] = d0 d00 − log ks|W k + δY |W
holds in E 1,1 (W ).
Proof. This can be checked locally on a trivialization U of L with a nowhere
vanishing sU ∈ Γ(U, L). Then there is a rational function f on X with s = f sU
and hence
     d0 d00 [− log ks|W ∩U an k] = d0 d00 [− log ksU |W ∩U an k] + d0 d00 [− log |f |W ∩U an |].
Using the definition of c1 (L|W ∩U an , k.k) for the first summand and using Theo-
rem 7.2 for the second summand, we get the claim.                               

       8. Piecewise smooth and formal metrics on line bundles
  In this section, X is an algebraic variety over K. In the following, we consider
an open subset W of X an .
  We first introduce piecewise smooth functions and piecewise linear functions
on W . This leads to corresponding notions for metrics on line bundles. We
prove that a piecewise linear metric is the same as a formal metric. We show
that canonical metrics in various situations are piecewise smooth.
   In Definition 1.6, we have defined piecewise smooth functions on an open
subset of an integral R-affine polyhedral set. Using tropicalizations and viewing
tropical varieties as polyhedral sets, we will define piecewise smooth functions
on W as follows:
Definition 8.1. A function f : W → R is called piecewise smooth if for every
x ∈ W there is a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) such that V is an open neighbourhood
of x in W and such that there is a piecewise smooth function φ on tropU (V )
with f = φ ◦ tropU on V .
   In a similar way, we will define a piecewise linear function on W . We recall
from Definition 1.6 that we have defined piecewise linear functions on integral
R-affine polyhedral complexes. As we are working with a variety over a valued
field, we will take the value group Γ into account and we will require in the def-
inition of piecewise linear functions additionally that the underlying polyhedral
complex and the restriction of the functions are both integral Γ-affine. Note
however that in Definition 8.1, the underlying polyhedral complex for φ is only
assumed to be integral R-affine.
Definition 8.2. A function f : W → R is called piecewise linear if for every
x ∈ W there is a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) such that V is an open neighbourhood
of x in W and a real function φ on tropU (V ) with f = φ ◦ tropU on V . We
require that there is an integral Γ-affine polyhedral complex Σ in NU,R with
tropU (V ) ⊆ |Σ| such that φ is the restriction of a function on |Σ| with integral
Γ-affine restrictions to all faces of Σ.
8.3. The space of piecewise smooth functions on W is an R-subalgebra of the
R-algebra of continuous functions on W . It contains all smooth functions on
W . The space of piecewise linear functions on W is closed under forming max
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                     65

and min. Moreover, it is a subgroup of the space of piecewise smooth functions
on W with respect to addition. If ϕ : X 0 → X is a morphism and W 0 is an
open subset of (ϕan )−1 (W ), then for every piecewise smooth (resp. piecewise
linear) function f on W , the restriction of f ◦ ϕ to W 0 is a piecewise smooth
(resp. piecewise linear) function on W 0 .
   In the following result, we need the G-topology on W . It is a Grothendieck
topology build up from analytic subdomains of W and it is closely related to
the Grothendieck topology of the underlying rigid analytic space ([Ber93, §1.3,
§1.6]).
Proposition 8.4. Let f : W → R be a continuous function. Then f is piecewise
smooth (resp. piecewise linear) if and only if there is a G-covering (Wi )i∈I
by analytic (resp. strict analytic) subdomains Wi of W and analytic moment
maps ϕi : Wi → (Ti )an to tori Ti := Spec(K[Mi ]) such that f = φi ◦ ϕi,trop
on Wi for a smooth (resp. integral Γ-affine) function φi : Ni,R → R, where
Ni := Hom(Mi , Z) as usual.
Proof. First, we assume that f is piecewise smooth (resp. piecewise linear).
For any x ∈ W , there is a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) in W containing x such that
f = φ ◦ tropU on V for a piecewise smooth (resp. integral Γ-affine function) φ
on the open subset Ω := tropU (V ) of Trop(U ). There are finitely many integral
R-affine
S         (resp. Γ-affine) polytopes ∆i in NU,R containing tropU (x) such that
   ∆
  i i  is a neighbourhood of tropU (x) in Ω and such that φ|∆i = φi |∆i for a
smooth (resp. integral Γ-affine) function φi : NU,R → R. Note that the affinoid
(resp. strictly affinoid) subdomains Wi (x) := trop−1  U (∆i ) of W contain x and
cover a neighbourhood of x. Letting x vary over W , we get a G-covering of W
with the desired properties.
   To prove the converse, we assume that f is given on a G-covering (Wi )i∈I of
W by smooth (resp. integral Γ-affine) functions φi : Ni,R → R with respect to
analytic moment maps ϕi : Wi → (Ti )an . Piecewise smoothness (resp. piecewise
linearity) is a local condition and so we have to check that f is piecewise smooth
in a neighbourhood of x ∈ X an . There is a finite I0 ⊆ I such that the sets
(Wi )i∈I0 cover a sufficiently small strict affinoid neighbourhood W 0 of x in W .
By shrinking W , we may assume that x ∈ Wi for every i ∈ I0 . In the following,
we restrict our attention to elements i ∈ I0 . The definition of an analytic (resp.
of a strict analytic) domain shows that we may assume that all Wi0 := Wi ∩ W 0
are affinoid (resp. strict affinoid) subdomains of W . Any analytic function on
a neighbourhood of x in Wi0 can be approximated uniformly on a sufficiently
small neighbourhood of x by rational functions on X. By shrinking W again,
this shows that we may assume that ϕi |Wi0 is induced by the restriction of an
algebraic moment map ϕ0i : Ui → Ti for a dense open subset Ui of X with
Wi0 ⊆ (Ui )an (see [Gub16, Prop. 7.2] for a similar argument). Similarly, we
may assume that there are affinoid coordinates (xij )j∈Ji on Wi0 which extend
to rational functions on X. Clearly, we may assume that |xij (x)| = 1 for
i ∈ IT0 and j ∈ Ji . There is a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) with x ∈ V ⊆ W 0 ,
U ⊆ i∈I0 Ui and such that all the functions xij are in O(U )× . We may assume
that tropU (x) = 0 and hence there is an open neighbourhood Ω    e of 0 in NU,R with
66                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

Definition 8.5. Let L be a line bundle on X and let W be an open subset of
X an . A metric k k on L|W is called piecewise smooth (resp. piecewise linear)
if for every x ∈ W , there is a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) with x ∈ V ⊆ W and a
nowhere vanishing section s ∈ Γ(U, L) such that − log ks|V k is piecewise smooth
(resp. piecewise linear) on V .
8.6. Since − log |f | is smooth for an invertible regular function f and even
the pull-back of a linear function with respect to a suitable tropicalization, the
last definition does neither depend on the choice of the trivialization s nor on
the choice of the tropical chart (V, ϕU ). Moreover, we may also use analytic
trivializations in the definition. By Proposition 8.4, the definition of a piecewise
linear metric agrees with the definition of PL-metrics in [CLD12, §6.2].
   Note that every piecewise linear metric is piecewise smooth. It follows from
8.3 that every piecewise smooth metric is continuous, that the tensor product
of piecewise linear (resp. piecewise smooth) metrics is again a piecewise linear
(resp. piecewise smooth) metric and that the dual metric of a a piecewise linear
(resp. piecewise smooth) metric is piecewise linear (resp. piecewise smooth).
Moreover, the pull-back of a piecewise linear (resp. piecewise smooth) metric
on L|W with respect to a morphism ϕ : X 0 → X is a piecewise linear (resp.
piecewise smooth) metric on ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 for any open subset W 0 of ϕ−1 (W ).
8.7. Recall that K ◦ is the valuation ring of the given non-archimedean absolute
value | | on K. Raynaud introduced an admissible formal scheme over K ◦ as
a formal scheme X over the valuation ring K ◦ which is locally isomorphic to
Spf(A) for a flat K ◦ -algebra A of topologically finite type over K ◦ (see [BL93,
§1] for details). For simplicity, we require additionally that X has a locally
finite atlas of admissible affine formal schemes over K ◦ . Then X has a generic
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                      67

fibre Xη (resp. a special fibre Xs ) which is a paracompact strictly analytic
Berkovich space over K (resp. an algebraic scheme over the residue field K̃)
locally isomorphic to M (A ) (resp. Spec(A ⊗K ◦ K̃)) for the strict affinoid
algebra A := A ⊗K ◦ K (see [Ber93, §1.6] for the equivalence to rigid analytic
spaces over K with an affinoid covering of finite type).
   A formal K ◦ -model of X is an admissible formal scheme X over K ◦ with
an isomorphism Xη ∼  = X an . For a line bundle L on X, we define a formal
K ◦ -model of L as a line bundle L on a formal K ◦ -model X of X with an
isomorphism Lη ∼ = Lan over Xη ∼= X an . For simplicity, we usually identify Lη
       an
with L .
8.8. Let L be a line bundle on X. A formal metric on L is a metric k kL
associated to a formal K ◦ -model L of L in the following way: If L admits
a formal trivialization over U and if s ∈ Γ(U , L ) corresponds under this
trivialization to the function γ ∈ OX (U ), then ks(x)k = |γ(x)| for all x ∈ Uη .
This definition is independent of the choice of the trivialization and shows
immediately that formal metrics are continuous. The tensor product and the
pull-back of formal metrics are again formal metrics.
Proposition 8.9. Every line bundle L on X has a formal K ◦ -model and hence
a formal metric.
Proof. This follows as in [Gub98, Prop. 7.6] based on the theorem of Raynaud
that every paracompact analytic space has a formal K ◦ -model (see [Bos14,
Theorem 8.4.3]). The argument for paracompact strictly K-analytic spaces
was first given in [CLD12, Proposition 6.2.13].                            
Proposition 8.10. Let k k be a formal metric on the line bundle L on X. Then
there is an admissible formal K ◦ -model X of X with reduced special fibre Xs
and a K ◦ -model L of L on X such that k k = k kL . Moreover, the invertible
sheaf associated to L is always canonically isomorphic to the sheaf on X given
by {s ∈ Γ(L, Uη ) | ||s(s)|| ≤ 1 ∀x ∈ Uη } on a formal open subset U of X .
Proof. This follows as in [Gub98, Lemma 7.4 and Proposition 7.5].                  
Proposition 8.11. Let k k be a metric on the line bundle L on X. Then the
following properties are equivalent:
    (a) k k is a formal metric;
    (b) k k is a piecewise linear metric;
    (c) there is a G-covering (Wi )i∈I of X an by strict analytic subdomains Wi of
        X an and trivializations si ∈ Γ(Wi , Lan ) with ksi (x)k = 1 for all x ∈ Wi
        and all i ∈ I.
Proof. If we use again Raynaud’s theorem to generalize to paracompact X an ,
the equivalence of (a) and (c) is proved as in [Gub98, Lemma 7.4 and Propo-
sition 7.5]. The implication (a) ⇒ (c) can also be found in [CLD12, Exemple
6.2.10]. It remains to see the equivalence of (b) and (c). Suppose that (b) holds.
Then there is a locally finite covering of X by trivializations Ui of L such that
− log ksi k is piecewise linear on (Ui )an for every i ∈ I. By Proposition 8.4, there
is a G-covering Wij of (Ui )an and analytic moment maps ϕij : Wij → (Tij )an
68                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

such that − log ksi k = φij ◦ ϕij,trop on Wij for an integral Γ-affine function φij
on Nij,R . The definition of integral Γ-affine functions shows that there is an
invertible analytic function γij on Wij such that ksi k = |γij | on Wij . Using the
                −1
trivialization γij si on Wij , we get (b) ⇒ (c). The converse is an immediate
application of Proposition 8.4.                                                  
8.12. If X is proper over K, than an algebraic K ◦ -model of X is an integral
scheme X which is of finite type, flat and proper over K ◦ and with a fixed
isomorphism between the generic fibre Xη and X. We use the isomorphism
to identify Xη and X. An algebraic K ◦ -model of L is a line bundle L on an
algebraic K ◦ -model X of X together with a fixed isomorphism between Lη and
L. We define an algebraic metric on L as in 8.8 by using an algebraic K ◦ -model
L of L.
Proposition 8.13. On a line bundle on a proper variety over K, a metric is
algebraic if and only if it is formal.
Proof. Passing to the formal completion along the special fibre, it is clear that
every algebraic metric is a formal metric. Using [Gub03, Proposition 10.5], the
converse is true if X is projective. The same argument shows that the converse
is also true for proper X if the formal GAGA theorem in [Gro61, Theorem 5.1.4]
holds over K ◦ and if X has an algebraic K ◦ -model. In [Gro61, Theorem 5.1.4],
the base has to be noetherian and hence it applies only for discrete valuation
rings. The required generalization is now given in [FK13, Theorem I.10.1.2].
The existence of an algebraic K ◦ -model follows from Nagata’s compactification
theorem. This was proved by Nagata in the noetherian case and proved by
Conrad in general (based on notes of Deligne, see Temkin’s paper [Tem11] for
another proof and references).                                                 
Corollary 8.14. Let L be a line bundle on a proper variety over K. Then L
has an algebraic metric.
Proof. This follows from Proposition 8.9 and Proposition 8.13.                    
     We will show now that many important metrics are piecewise smooth.
Example 8.15. Let L be a line bundle on the abelian variety A over K.
Choosing a rigidification of L at 0 ∈ A and assuming L symmetric (resp. odd),
                                                                    2
the theorem of the cube allows to identify [m]∗ (L) with L⊗m (resp. L⊗m ).
                                                                                   2
There is a unique continuous metric k kcan on Lan with [m]∗ k kcan = k k⊗m     can
(resp. [m]∗ k kcan = k k⊗m
                         can ) for all m ∈ Z. In general, L
                                                           ⊗2 is the tensor product

of a symmetric and an odd line bundle, unique up to 2-torsion in Pic(A), and
hence we get a canonical metric k kcan on L which is unique up to multiples
from |K × | if we vary rigidifications. We claim that k kcan is locally on X an the
tensor product of a smooth metric and a piecewise linear metric. In particular,
we deduce that k kcan is a piecewise smooth metric.
   To prove the claim, we use the Raynaud extension of A to describe the
canonical metric on L (see [Gub10, §4] for details). The Raynaud extension is
an exact sequence
                                             q
(8.15.1)                 1 −→ T an −→ E −→ B an −→ 0
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                   69

 of commutative analytic groups, where T = Spec(K[M ]) is a multiplicative
torus of rank r and B is an abelian variety of good reduction. Moreover, there
is a lattice P in E with E/P = Aan . More precisely P is a discrete subgroup of
E(K) which is mapped by a canonical map val : E → NR isomorphically onto a
complete lattice Λ of NR , where N is the dual of M . The map val is locally over
B a tropicalization. Note that the Raynaud extension is algebraizable, but the
quotient homomorphism p : E → Aan is only defined in the analytic category.
   Let B be the abelian scheme over K ◦ with generic fibre B. By loc. cit.
there exists a line bundle H on B such that q ∗ ((Hη )an ) = p∗ (Lan ). Here
and in the following, we use rigidified line bundles to identify isomorphic line
bundles. Then q ∗ k kH is a formal metric on p∗ (Lan ). On p∗ (Lan ), we have a
canonical P -action α over the canonical action of P on E by translation. By
loc. cit. there is a 1-cocycle Z in Z 1 (P, (R× )E ) such that
(8.15.2)            (q ∗ kαγ (w)kH )γ·x = Zγ (x)−1 · (q ∗ kwkH )x
for all γ ∈ P , x ∈ E and w ∈ (p∗ Lan )x . The cocycle Z depends only on the
map val which means that there is a unique function zλ : NR → R with
             zλ (val(x)) = − log(Zγ (x))   (γ ∈ P, x ∈ E, λ = val(γ)).
Moreover, there is a canonical symmetric bilinear form b : Λ×Λ → Z associated
to L such that
                   zλ (ω) = zλ (0) + b(ω, λ)   (ω ∈ NR , λ ∈ Λ).
The cocycle condition
                   Zργ (x) = Zρ (γx)Zγ (x)     (ρ, γ ∈ P, x ∈ E)
shows that
                 zλ+µ (0) = zλ (0) + zµ (0) + b(λ, µ)   (λ, µ ∈ Λ)
which means that λ 7→ zλ (0) is a quadratic function on Λ. There is a unique
extension to a quadratic function q0 : NR → R. We define a metric k k on
p∗ (Lan ) by k k := e−q0 ◦val q ∗ k kH . Using (8.15.2) and that q0 is a quadratic
function with associated bilinear form b, it follows easily that k k descends to
the canonical metric on L. We conclude from the descent with respect to the
local isomorphism p that the canonical metric on L is locally on Aan the tensor
product of a smooth metric with a piecewise linear metric. This proves the
claim.
Example 8.16. Let L be a line bundle on a proper smooth algebraic variety
over K which is algebraically equivalent to zero. Let A denote the Albanese
variety of X (see [Gro62, Exp. 236, Thm. 2.1, Cor. 3.2]). We fix some
x ∈ X(K) and obtain a universal morphism ψ : X → A from X to the abelian
variety A with ψ(x) = 0. Furthermore L is in a canonical way the pullback of
an odd line bundle on A along ψ. It follows that L carries a canonical metric
k kcan , unique up to multiples from |K × |. By [Gub10, Example 3.7], there is
an integer N ≥ 1 such that k k⊗N can is an algebraic metric and hence piecewise
linear. We conclude that k kcan is a piecewise smooth metric.
70                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

Example 8.17. Let L be a line bundle on a complete toric variety X over K.
Similarly as in the case of abelian varieties and using rigidifications, we have
[m]∗ (L) = L⊗m and there is a unique metric k kcan on L with [m]∗ k kcan =
k k⊗m
    can for all integers m ∈ Z (see [Mai00, Section 3]). There is a canonical
algebraic K ◦ -model X of K ◦ and a canonical algebraic K ◦ -model L by using
the same rational polyhedral fan and the same piecewise linear function. Since
k kcan = k kL , the canonical metric on L is algebraic and hence a piecewise
linear metric.
8.18. Finally, we consider the case where our variety X is defined over a ground
field F which is equipped with the trivial valuation. If L is a line bundle on
X, then we choose an algebraically closed extension field K endowed with a
non-trivial complete absolute value extending the trivial absolute value of F .
Then F ⊆ K ◦ and the line bundle L ⊗F K ◦ on X ⊗F K ◦ is a canonical algebraic
K ◦ -model of the line bundle LK on XK . We conclude that L has a canonical
metric k kcan .
   The metric k kcan has the following intrinsic description. Let U = Spec(A) be
an affine open subset of X which is a trivialization of L given by the nowhere
vanishing section s ∈ Γ(U, L). We consider the formal affinoid subdomain
U ◦ := {x ∈ U an | |f (x)| ≤ 1 ∀f ∈ A} of X an . Note that U ◦ is the set of points
in U an with reduction contained in U (see [Gub13, §4] for more details). It
follows that ks(x)kcan = 1 for all x ∈ U ◦ . Since X is proper, such trivializations
U ◦ cover X an leading to a description of k kcan which is independent of K.
   For simplicity, we have considered only varieties in this paper. We may also
consider continuous metrics on Lan for a line bundle over a separated scheme
X of finite type over the ground field F . For such schemes X, the intrinsic
description above shows in particular that we still have a canonical metric
k kcan on L in the case of a trivially valued F .

             9. Piecewise smooth forms and delta-metrics
   We consider again an algebraic variety X over K of dimension n. In this
section, we first study piecewise smooth forms on an open subset W of X an .
This leads to a decomposition of the first Chern current of a piecewise smoothly
metrized line bundle (L|W , k k) into the sum of a piecewise smooth form and a
residual current. We show that the residual current is induced by a generalized
δ-form. If the first Chern current of (L|W , k k) is induced by a δ-form on W ,
then k k is called a δ-metric and the δ-form is called the first Chern δ-form. We
show that many important metrics are δ-metrics. In the following sections, we
will use δ-metrics for our approach to non-archimedean Arakelov theory.
9.1. In Definition 3.10, we have defined the space P S(Ω) of piecewise smooth
superforms on an open subset Ω of a polyhedral subset. If (V, ϕU ) is a tropical
chart, then we apply this definition for the open subset Ω := tropU (V ) of
Trop(U ). If α ∈ P S(Ω) and (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) is a tropical chart with V 0 ⊆ V and
U 0 ⊆ U , then we define α|V 0 as the piecewise smooth form on Ω0 := tropU 0 (V 0 )
given by pull-back of α with respect to the canonical affine map NU 0 ,R → NU,R .
      A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                          71

Definition 9.2. A piecewise smooth form on an open subset W of X an may be
defined in a similar way as a differential form in A(W ): A piecewise smooth form
α is given by an open covering (Vi , ϕUi )i∈I of W by tropical charts and piecewise
smooth superforms αi on Ωi := tropUi (Vi ) such that αi |Vi ∩Vj = αj |Vi ∩Vj for all
i, j ∈ I. A superform α0 given by the covering (Vj0 , ϕUj0 )j∈J and piecewise
smooth superforms αj0 on Ω0j := tropUj0 (Vj0 ) will be identified with α if and only
if αi |Vi ∩Vj0 = αj0 |Vi ∩Vj0 for every i ∈ I and every j ∈ J.
9.3. We denote the space of piecewise smooth forms on W by P S(W ). It comes
with a bigrading and is canonically equipped with a ∧-product. We conclude
easily that P S ·,· (W ) is a bigraded A·,· (W )-algebra on X an . It is clear that
P S 0,0 (W ) is the space of piecewise smooth functions on W . It coincides with
the space P 0,0 (W ) of generalized δ-preforms of degree zero. The equality
(9.3.1)                          P S 0,0 (W ) = P 0,0 (W )
is in fact a direct consequence of (4.19.3).
   If ϕ : X 0 → X is a morphism of algebraic varieties over K, then the pull-
back of piecewise smooth superforms from 3.10 carries over to define a pull-back
f ∗ : P S p,q (W ) → P S p,q (W 0 ) for any open subset W 0 of (X 0 )an with f (W 0 ) ⊆ W .
In the special case of X = X 0 , f = id and W 0 an open subset of W , we denote
the pull-back by α|W 0 and call it the restriction of α to W 0 .
9.4. In 3.11.1, we have introduced differentials of piecewise smooth forms on
open subsets of polyhedral sets. If α ∈ P S p,q (W ) is given as in Definition
9.2, then the polyhedral differential d0P α ∈ P S p+1,q (W ) is locally defined by
d0P αi ∈ P S p+1,q (Ωi ). Similarly, we define d00P α ∈ P S p,q+1 (W ). Then P S ·,· (W )
is a differential graded R-algebra with respect to the polyhedral differentials d0P
and d00P .
9.5. The bigraded differential R-algebras P S(W ) of piecewise smooth forms
and P (W ) of generalized δ-forms are not directly comparable except that they
contain both A(W ) as a bigraded differential R-subalgebra. We construct a
bigraded differential R-algebra P SP (W ) containing both spaces as follows.
   Recall from Remark 3.14 that we have obtained a bigraded differential R-
algebra P SP (Ω)e with respect to d0 , d00 for any open subset Ω  e of NR . We
                                       P P
repeat now the construction of generalized δ-forms in §4 building up on the
spaces P SP (Ω)e instead of P (Ω).
                                e     This leads first to spaces P SP (V, ϕU ) for
tropical charts (V, ϕU ) of X and then to the desired space P SP (W ). Note that
P SP (W ) is a differential bigraded R-algebra with respect to the polyhedral
differential operators d0P and d00P which extends the corresponding structure on
the subalgebra P (W ). To see that P S(W ) is a graded subalgebra of P SP (W ),
we use the obvious generalization of Proposition 1.8 from piecewise smooth
functions to piecewise smooth forms. Obviously, P SP (W ) is generated by the
subalgebras P S(W ) and P (W ). Moreover, the polyhedral differentials d0P , d00P
agree with the corresponding differential operators on P S(W ).
   All properties of generalized δ-forms from §4R and §5 extend immediately to
the sheaves P SP . Hence we have an integral W α for any α ∈ P SPcn,n (W ).
As a special case, we obtain such an integral for a piecewise smooth form with
72                    WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

compact support on W . As in 6.4, this leads to a δ-current [α] ∈ E p,q (W ) for
any α ∈ P SP p,q (W ). In particular, this applies to a piecewise smooth α.
Remark 9.6. Note that the polyhedral differential d0P α of a piecewise smooth
form α or more generally of any α ∈ P SP (W ) is not compatible with the
corresponding differential of the associated δ-current. We define the d0 -residue
by
                           Resd0 (α) := d0 [α] − [d0P α].
Similarly, we define residues with respect to d00 and d0 d00 .
9.7. Now we consider a line bundle L on X endowed with a piecewise smooth
metric k k over the open subset W of X an . We are going to obtain a canon-
ical decomposition of the Chern current [c1 (L|W , k k)] ∈ E 1,1 (X an ) (see 7.7)
into a piecewise smooth part c1 (L|W , k k)ps ∈ P S 1,1 (W ) and a residual part
[c1 (L|W , k k)]res ∈ E 1,1 (W ).
   Let (U, s) be a trivialization of L, i.e. U is an open subset of X and s is
a nowhere vanishing section in Γ(U, L). Then − log ksk is a piecewise smooth
function on U an ∩ W and hence −d0P d00P log ks|U an ∩W k ∈ P S 1,1 (U an ∩ W ). Note
that this piecewise smooth form is independent of the choice of s by the same
argument as in 7.7 and hence we obtain a globally defined element of P S 1,1 (W )
which we denote by c1 (L|W , k k)ps . Recall from 9.5 that we denote the as-
sociated δ-current on W by [c1 (L|W , k k)ps ]. The same argument shows that
the residues Resd0 d00 (− log ks|U an ∩W k) paste together to give a global δ-current
[c1 (L|W , k k)]res ∈ E 1,1 (W ) and we have
(9.7.1)        [c1 (L|W , k k)] = [c1 (L|W , k k)ps ] + [c1 (L|W , k k)]res .
Proposition 9.8. Let k k be a piecewise smooth metric on L|W . Then there
is a unique β ∈ P 1,1 (W ) with
                   [ϕ∗ (β)] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)]res ∈ E 1,1 (W 0 )
for every morphism ϕ : X 0 → X from any algebraic variety X 0 over K and for
every open subset W 0 of ϕ−1 (W ). The generalized δ-form β has codimension 1
(see Definition 4.13) and will be denoted by c1 (L|W , k k)res .
Proof. Note that uniqueness follows from Proposition 6.8. By definition of
a piecewise smooth metric, there is an open covering (Vi )i∈I of W by tropi-
cal charts (Vi , ϕUi ), nowhere vanishing sections si ∈ Γ(Vi , Lan ) and piecewise
smooth functions φi on Ωi := tropUi (Vi ) with − log ksi k = φi ◦ tropUi on Vi .
Passing to a refinement of the open covering, we may assume that φi is defined
on Trop(Ui ). By Proposition 1.8, there is a piecewise smooth function φ̃i on
NUi ,R restricting to φi . By Lemma 1.12, the corner locus Ci := φ̃i · NUi ,R of φ̃i
is a tropical cycle of codimension 1.
   The δ-preform δCi represents an element βi ∈ P (Vi , ϕUi ) of codimension 1
(see 4.4). We have seen in 4.5 that there is a pull-back f ∗ (βi ) ∈ P 1,1 (V 0 , ϕU 0 )
for every morphism f : X 0 → X of algebraic varieties over K and every tropical
chart (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) of X 0 compatible with (Vi , ϕUi ). For the open subset Ω0 :=
tropU 0 (V 0 ) of Trop(U 0 ), we have f ∗ (βi )|Ω0 ∈ P 1,1 (Ω0 ) ⊆ D1,1 (Ω0 ) (see 4.5.1).
Let F : NU 0 ,R → NUi ,R be the canonical affine map with tropUi = F ◦ tropU 0 on
      A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                           73

(U 0 )an . By Proposition 1.14 and Corollary 1.15, F ∗ (Ci ) · Trop(U 0 ) is the corner
locus of φ0 := φi ◦ F |Trop(U 0 ) and hence we get
             f ∗ (βi )|Ω0 = F ∗ (δCi ) ∧ δTrop(U 0 ) = δφ0 ·Trop(U 0 ) ∈ P 1,1 (Ω0 ).
Together with the tropical Poincaré–Lelong formula (Corollary 3.19), we get
(9.8.1)                   f ∗ (βi )|Ω0 + [d0P d00P φ0 ] = d0 d00 [φ0 ] ∈ D1,1 (Ω0 ).
It follows from (9.8.1) that f ∗ (βi )|Ω0 is independent of all choices. This yields
that βi |Vi ∩Vj = βj |Vi ∩Vj for all i, j ∈ I. We get a well-defined generalized δ-form
β ∈ P 1,1 (W ) of codimension 1 given by βi ∈ P 1,1 (Vi , ϕUi ) on Vi for every i ∈ I.
    It remains to check that [ϕ∗ (β)] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)]res for every mor-
phism ϕ : X 0 → X and every open subset W 0 of ϕ−1 (W ). This has to be tested
on α ∈ Bcn−1,n−1 (W 0 ). The claim is local and a partition of unity argument
in a paracompact open neighbourhood of supp(α) shows that we may assume
supp(α) ⊆ ϕ−1 (Vi ) for some i ∈ I. There are finitely many tropical charts
(Vj0 , ϕUj0 )j∈J in W 0 which cover supp(α) such that α is given on every Vj0 by
αj ∈ AZ n−1,n−1 (Vj0 , ϕUj0 ). We choose a non-empty very affine open subset U 0
of X 0 contained in every Uj0 and in ϕ−1 (Ui ). By Proposition 5.9, U 0 is a very

Definition 9.9. A metric k k on L|W is called a δ-metric if for every x ∈ W ,
there are a tropical chart (V, ϕU ) such that x ∈ V ⊆ W and a piecewise smooth
function φ on Trop(U ) satisfying the following properties:
    (i) There is a nowhere vanishing section s of L over U such that φ◦tropU =
        − log ksk on V .
   (ii) There is a superform γ on NU,R of bidegree (1, 1) with piecewise smooth
        coefficients such that d0P d00P φ and γ|Trop(U ) agree on the open subset
        tropU (V ) of Trop(U ).
Remark 9.10. Condition (i) just means that the metric is piecewise smooth.
Note that a superform on NU,R with piecewise smooth coefficients is the same
as a δ-preform on NU,R of codimension 0 (see Example 2.10). Using 9.7, we
deduce easily that (ii) is equivalent to the condition that [c1 (L|W , k k)ps ] is the
δ-current associated to a generalized δ-form on W (of codimension 0).
Proposition 9.11. Let k k be a piecewise smooth metric on L|W . Then k k is
a δ-metric if and only if there is a β ∈ B 1,1 (W ) with
                     [ϕ∗ (β)] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)] ∈ E 1,1 ((W 0 )
for every morphism ϕ : X 0 → X from any algebraic variety X 0 over K and for
every open subset W 0 of ϕ−1 (W ).
Proof. Suppose that k k is a δ-metric. By Remark 9.10, there is γ ∈ P 1,1 (W )
of codimension 0 such that [c1 (L|W , k k)ps ] = [γ]. Since γ is of codimension 0,
we may handle γ as a piecewise smooth form and hence we get
      [ϕ∗ (γ)] = [ϕ∗ (c1 (L|W , k k)ps )] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)ps ] ∈ E 1,1 (W 0 ).
Proposition 9.8 yields that β := c1 (L, k k)res + γ ∈ P 1,1 (W ) and that
[ϕ∗ (β)] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)res ] + [ϕ∗ (γ)] = [c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k)] ∈ E 1,1 (W 0 )
as claimed. It remains to show that β ∈ B 1,1 (W ). Let (V, ϕU ) be a tropical
chart in W and let φ be a piecewise smooth function on Trop(U ) as in Defini-
tion 9.9 such that β|V is given by βV ∈ P 1,1 (V, ϕU ). For every tropical chart
(U 0 , ϕU 0 ) of an algebraic variety X 0 over K compatible with (V, ϕU ) with re-
spect to the morphism f : X 0 → X and for Ω0 := tropU 0 (V 0 ), the last display
yields
(9.11.1)                 [f ∗ (βV )|Ω0 ] = d0 d00 [φ ◦ F ] ∈ D1,1 (Ω0 ),
where F : NU 0 ,R → NU,R is the canonical affine map. Since this supercurrent is
d0 -closed and d00 -closed on Ω0 , we conclude that β is given on V by an element
of Z(V, ϕU ). This shows β ∈ B 1,1 (W ).
    To prove the converse, we just use that [c1 (L|W , k k)] = [β] for some β ∈
P 1,1 (W ). By Proposition 9.8, [c1 (L|W , k k)ps ] is the δ-current associated to
β − c1 (L|W , k k)res ∈ P 1,1 (W ). By Remark 9.10, k k is a δ-metric.          
Definition 9.12. Let k k be a δ-metric on L|W . By Proposition 6.8, the δ-form
β in Proposition 9.11 is unique. We call it the first Chern δ-form of (L|W , k k)
and we denote it by c1 (L|W , k k).
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                        75

9.13. We summarize the above constructions and definitions. A metric k k
on L|W is a δ-metric if and only if every x ∈ W is contained in a tropical
chart (V, ϕU ) in W with a piecewise smooth function φ on NU,R and a nowhere
vanishing section s of L over U such that
                                − log ksk = φ ◦ tropU
on V and such that
                           d0P d00P (φ|tropU (V ) ) = γ|tropU (V )
for a superform γ on NR of bidegree (1, 1) with piecewise smooth coefficients.
Then the restriction of c1 (L|W , k k)res to V is represented by the δ-preform
δφ·NU,R on NU,R , c1 (L|W , k k)ps |V is given by γ and c1 (L|W , k k)|V is represented
by the δ-preform γ + δφ·NU,R on NU,R . A piecewise linear metric is a δ-metric
as we can choose φ integral Γ-affine (use Remark 1.9) and γ = 0.
9.14. By construction, the δ-current associated to c1 (L|W , k k) is equal to the
first Chern current [c1 (L|W , k k)] defined in 7.7 which explains the notation
used there. It is an immediate consequence of equation (9.11.1) that the first
Chern δ-form c1 (L|W , k k) is d0 -closed and d00 -closed.
   To be a δ-metric is a local property and respects isometry. The tensor product
of δ-metrics is again a δ-metric and the dual metric of a δ-metric is also a δ-
metric. If a positive tensor power of a metric k k on L|W is a δ-metric, then
k k is a δ-metric. It is easy to see that the first Chern δ-form c1 (L|W , k k) is
additive in terms of isometry classes (L|W , k k) for δ-metrics k k.
Proposition 9.15. Let ϕ : X 0 → X be a morphism of algebraic varieties and
let L be a line bundle on X endowed with a δ-metric k k over the open subset
W of X an . Then ϕ∗ k k is a δ-metric on ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 and we have
(9.15.1)            c1 (ϕ∗ (L)|W 0 , ϕ∗ k k) = ϕ∗ c1 (L|W , k k)∈ B 1,1 (W 0 )
for any open subset W 0 of ϕ−1 (W ).
Proof. This follows from 8.6 and Proposition 9.11.                                   
Remark 9.16. Smooth metrics and piecewise linear metrics are δ-metrics
which is clear from the definitions. It follows from Proposition 8.11 that ev-
ery formal metric is a δ-metric. In particular, every algebraic metric on a line
bundle of a proper variety is a δ-metric.
Example 9.17. All the canonical metrics in Example 8.15, in Example 8.16
and in Example 8.17 are δ-metrics. Indeed, a positive tensor power of such a
metric is locally the tensor product of a formal metric with a smooth metric
and hence the claim follows from Remark 9.16.

                         10. Monge-Ampère measures
   We have seen in the previous section that formal metrics are δ-metrics giving
rise to a first Chern δ-form. The formalism of δ-forms allows us to define the
Monge-Ampère measure as a wedge-product of first Chern δ-forms. We recall
that Chambert–Loir has introduced discrete measures for formally metrized line
76                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

bundles on a proper variety which are important for non-archimedean equidistri-
bution. The main result of this section shows that the Monge–Ampère measure
is equal to the Chambert–Loir measure.
   In this section X is a proper algebraic variety over K of dimension n.
10.1. Let L1 , . . . , Ln be line bundles on X endowed with δ-metrics. Then the
wedge-product c1 (L1 ) ∧ · · · ∧ c1 (Ln ) of the first Chern δ-forms is a δ-form of
bidegree (n, n). By Corollary 6.15, the δ-current associated to a δ-form on X an
of type (n, n) extends to a bounded linear functional on the space of continuous
functions and defines a signed Radon measure on X an . The signed measure
associated to c1 (L1 ) ∧ · · · ∧ c1 (Ln ) is called the Monge-Ampère measure and is
denoted by                                                   
                                MA c1 (L1 ), . . . , c1 (Ln ) .
Proposition 10.2. If ϕ : X 0 → X is a morphism of n-dimensional proper
varieties over K, then the following projection formula holds:
                                                                                  
       ϕ∗ MA c1 (ϕ∗ L1 ), . . . , c1 (ϕ∗ Ln ) = deg(ϕ)MA c1 (L1 ), . . . , c1 (Ln ) .
Proof. The Stone-Weierstraß Theorem [CLD12, Prop. (3.3.5)] implies that
A0 (X an ) is a dense subspace of C(X an ). For functions in A0 (X an ) the de-
sired equality follows from Proposition 9.15 and from the projection formula
for δ-forms (5.9.1). This yields our claim.                                  
Proposition 10.3. If X is a proper variety of dimension n, then the total mass
of MA c1 (L1 , k k1 ), . . . , c1 (Ln , k kn ) is equal to degL1 ,...,Ln (X).
Proof. This follows similarly as in [CLD12, Proposition 6.4.3]. They handled
there only the case of smooth metrics, but our formalism of δ-forms allows us
to obtain this result more generally for δ-metrics.                        
   We recall the crucial properties of Chambert-Loir’s measures. They were
introduced in a slightly different setting by Chambert–Loir in [CL06].
Proposition 10.4. There is a unique way to associate to any n-dimensional
proper variety X over K and to any family of formally metrized line bundles
L1 , . . . , Ln on X a signed Radon measure µ = µL1 ,...,Ln on X an such that the
following properties hold:
     (a) The measure µ is multilinear and symmetric in L1 , . . . , Ln .
     (b) If ϕ : Y → X is a morphism of n-dimensional proper varieties over K,
             then the following projection formula holds:
                                              
                           ϕ∗ µϕ∗ L1 ,...,ϕ∗ Ln = deg(ϕ)µL1 ,...,Ln .
     (c) If X is a formal K ◦ -model of X with reduced special fibre Xs and if
         the metric of Lj is induced by a formal K ◦ -model Lj of Lj on X for
         every j = 1, . . . , n, then
                                    X
                                µ=    degL1 ,...,Ln (Y )δξY ,
                                 Y
        where Y ranges over the irreducible components of Xs and δξY is the
        Dirac measure in the unique point ξY of X an which reduces to the generic
        point of Y (see [Ber90, Proposition 2.4.4]).
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                    77

   (d) The total mass is given by µ(X an ) = degL1 ,...,Ln (X).
Proof. For existence, we refer to [Gub07, §3]. Uniqueness follows from (c) alone
as the existence of a simultaneous formal K ◦ -model with reduced special fibre
is a consequence of [Gub98, Proposition 7.5].                                 
Theorem 10.5. For formally metrized line bundles L1 , . . . , Ln on the proper
variety X of dimension n, the Monge-Ampère measure MA(c1 (L1 ), . . . , c1 (Ln ))
agrees with the Chambert-Loir measure µL1 ,...,Ln .
   This theorem was first proven by Chambert-Loir and Ducros [CLD12, §6.9]
for their Monge-Ampère measures defined by a tricky approximation process
with smooth metrics. Their argument uses Zariski–Riemann spaces, while we
use here a more tropical approach related to our δ-forms.
10.6. In Lemma 10.8, we will consider a closed subvariety U of a torus T =
Spec(K ◦ [M ]) over K ◦ . We will use the following notation: N is the dual of the
free abelian group M of finite rank. Let U be the generic fibre of U and let Us
be the special fibre.
   The tropicalization trop : (TK )an → NR (resp. trop : Tan     s → NR ) with
respect to the valuation v on K (resp. the trivial valuation on K̃) leads to the
tropical variety Trop(U ) (resp. Trop(Us )).
   The local cone LC0 (Trop(U )) at 0 is defined as the cone in NR which agrees
with Trop(U ) in a neighbourhood of 0. We endow it with the weights induced
by the canonical tropical weights on Trop(U ).
10.7. For the proof of Theorem 10.5, we need a preparatory result. Let L be
a line bundle on the proper variety X over K. We consider an algebraic K ◦ -
model (X , L ) of (X, L). Then we get an algebraic metric k kL on L. We have
seen in 8.18 that the restriction Ls of L to the special fibre Xs has a canonical
metric k kcan . Note that the first metric is continuous on the Berkovich space
X an with respect to the given valuation v while k kcan is continuous on the
Berkovich space Xsan with respect to the trivial valuation on the residue field
K̃. Since X is assumed to be proper, we have a reduction map π : X an → Xs .
For x ∈ X an , π(x) is a scheme theoretic point of Xs . Using the trivial valuation
on the residue field of π(x), we will view π(x) as a point of Xsan .
   In the next lemma, we will show that k kcan is piecewise linear in an neigh-
bourhood of π(x) in Xsan . This means that using a trivialization and a tropi-
calization, the canonical metric is induced by a piecewise linear function on the
tropical variety. It will be crucial in the proof of Theorem 10.5 that we can
use tropically the same piecewise linear function to describe the formal metric
k kL in a neighbourhood of x in X an . We make this now precise:
Lemma 10.8. Under the setup given in 10.7, we fix an element x ∈ X an and
an open neighbourhood V of π(x) in X . Then there is an open neighbourhood
U of π(x) in V and a closed embedding U ,→ T into a torus T = Spec(K ◦ [M ])
with the following properties (a)–(f ) using the notation from 10.6:
    (a) We have 0 = trop(x) and the weighted local cone in 0 satisfies
                           LC0 (Trop(U )) = Trop(Us ).
78                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN


     (b) There is an open neighbourhood Ωe of 0 in NR with
                                        e = Trop(U ) ∩ e
                       LC0 (Trop(U )) ∩ Ω              Ω.
     (c) There exist a complete rational polyhedral fan Σ on NR and a continuous
         function φ : NR → R which is piecewise linear with respect to Σ (i.e.
         for every σ ∈ Σ, there is uσ ∈ M with φ = uσ on σ).
    (d) U is a trivialization of L with respect to a nowhere vanishing section
         s ∈ Γ(U , L ).
     (e) We have − log kskL = φ ◦ trop on a neighbourhood of x in X an .
     (f) We have − log kskcan = φ ◦ trop on a neighbourhood of π(x) in Xsan .
If π(x) is the generic point of an irreducible component of Xs , then there is an
U as above with (a)–(d) and the following stronger properties:
    (e’) We have − log kskL = φ ◦ trop on trop−1 ( e Ω) ⊆ U an .
    (f’) The identity − log kskcan = φ ◦ trop holds on Usan .
Proof. Let (Ui )i∈I be a finite affine open covering of X such that L is trivial
over any Ui . The generic (resp. special) fibre of Ui is denoted by Ui (resp.
Ui,s ). For every i ∈ I, we choose a nowhere vanishing section si ∈ Γ(Ui , L ).
Let I(x) := {i ∈ I | π(x) ∈ Ui,s }. For i ∈ I(x), let (xij )j∈Ji be a finite set of
generators of the K ◦ -algebra O(Ui ). Replacing xij by 1 + xij if necessary, we
may assume that these generators are invertible in π(x). For i ∈ I, we have an
affinoid subdomain
       Ui◦ := {z ∈ Uian | |a(z)| ≤ 1 ∀a ∈ O(Ui )} = {z ∈ Uian | π(z) ∈ Ui,s }
of X an . Using the trivial valuation on K̃, we get similarly an affinoid subdomain
Ui,s
   ◦ := {z ∈ U an | |a(z)| ≤ 1 ∀a ∈ O(U )} of X an . We consider π(x) as a
                    i,s                        i,s       s
point of Xsan by using the trivial absolute value on the residue field of π(x) and
hence we have I(x) = {i ∈ I | π(x) ∈ Ui,s    ◦ }.

   It is easy to see that π(x) has a very affine open neighbourhood U in X such
that U is contained in Ui for every i ∈ I(x). Very affine means that there is a

  Now we are ready to prove Theorem 10.5.
Proof. Let µMA := MA(c1 (L1 ), . . . , c1 (Ln )). For simplicity, we assume that
L = L1 = · · · = Ln and that all metrics are induced by the same K ◦ -model L on
X . The general case follows either by the same arguments or by multilinearity.
It is more convenient for us to work algebraically and so we use Proposition
8.13 to assume that X and L are algebraic K ◦ -models. There is a generically
finite surjective morphism X 0 → X from a proper flat variety X 0 over K ◦ with
reduced special fibre. This is a consequence of de Jong’s pluristable alteration
theorem which works over any Henselian valuation ring (see [Ber99, Lemma
9.2]). Since both sides of the claim satisfy the projection formula, we may
prove the claim for X 0 . This shows that we may assume that X is an algebraic
K ◦ -model of X with reduced special fibre.
   We will analyse µMA in a neighbourhood of x ∈ X an . Let π(x) ∈ Xs be the
reduction of x. We choose a very affine open neighbourhood U of π(x) in X
80                   WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

as in Lemma 10.8. We will use the closed embedding U ,→ T into the torus T
and the notation from there.
   It follows from a theorem of Ducros [Duc12, Théorème 3.4] that x has a
compact analytic neighbourhood V such that the germ of trop(V ) in trop(x)
(considering polytopal neighbourhoods) agrees with the germ of trop(W ) in
trop(x) for every compact analytic neighbourhood W ⊆ V of x. Using that
trop(x) = 0, we deduce from 1) in [Duc12, Théorème 3.4] that the dimension
of the germ is equal to the transcendence degree of K̃(π(x)) over K̃.
   We first assume that π(x) is not the generic point of an irreducible com-
ponent of Xs . Then the transcendence degree of K̃(π(x)) over K̃ is smaller
than n = dim(X). Using the theorem of Ducros, there is a compact analytic
neighbourhood V of x in X an such that trop(V ) has dimension < n and such
that Lemma 10.8(e) holds on V . We choose a tropical chart (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) in x
which is contained in V and with U 0 contained in the generic fibre U of U .
We describe c1 (L)|V 0 using the function φ constructed in Lemma 10.8 and the
canonical affine map F : NU 0 ,R → NR . Using that φ is piecewise linear, it
follows from 9.13 that c1 (L)|V 0 = trop∗U 0 (β) for β ∈ AZ 1,1 (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) represented
by the δ-preform δF ∗ (φ)·NU 0 ,R on NU 0 ,R . By our construction of products and
Corollary 1.15, µMA is given on V 0 by β ∧n ∈ AZ n,n (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) represented by the
δ-preform
                                                ∧n
(10.8.5)                        δF ∗ (φ)·NU 0 ,R     = δF ∗ (C)
on NU 0 ,R , where C is the n-codimensional tropical cycle of NR obtained by the
n-fold self-intersection of the tropical divisor φ · NR . Since V 0 ⊆ V , we have
F (tropU 0 (V 0 )) ⊆ trop(V ) and hence dim(F (tropU 0 (V 0 ))) < n. It follows from
the definition of the pull-back and the local nature of stable tropical intersection
that δF ∗ (C)·Trop(U 0 ) does not meet the open subset tropU 0 (V 0 ) of Trop(U 0 ). A
similar argument applies to any tropical chart compatible with (V 0 , ϕU 0 ) and
hence (10.8.5) yields β ∧n = 0. We conclude that the support of µMA does not
meet V 0 .
   Now we assume that π(x) is the generic point of an irreducible component

                               11. Green currents
  In this section X is an algebraic variety over K of dimension n. We introduce
Green currents for cycles on X. We define the product gY ∗ gZ for a divisor Y
and a cycle Z on X which intersect properly. This operation has the expected
properties.
Definition 11.1. Let Z be a cycle of X of codimension p and let g be any
δ-current in E p−1,p−1 (X an ). Then we define ω(Z, g) := d0 d00 g + δZ ∈ E p,p (X an ).
If there is a δ-form ωZ,g ∈ B p,p (X an ) with ω(Z, g) = [ωZ,g ], then we call g a
Green current for the cycle Z. We will use often the notion gZ for such a current
and then we set ω(gZ ) := ω(Z, gZ ) and ωZ := ωZ,gZ for simplicity.
11.2. Let (L, k k) be a line bundle on X endowed with a δ-metric and let Z
be a cycle of codimension p in X with any current gZ ∈ E p−1,p−1 (X an ). We
assume that s is a meromorphic section of L with Cartier divisor D intersecting
Z properly. By the Poincaré–Lelong equation in Corollary 7.8 and by the
definition of a δ-metric in 9.12, gY := [− log ksk] is a Green current for the Weil
divisor Y associated to D with ωY = c1 (L, k k).
   If Z is a prime cycle of codimension p, then we define gY ∧ δZ ∈ E p,p (X an )
as the push-forward of [− log ksk|Z ] with respect to the inclusion iZ : Z → X.
82                   WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

In general, we proceed by linearity in the prime components of Z to define
gY ∧ δZ ∈ E p,p (X an ). This leads to the definition of the ∗-product
                   gY ∗ gZ := gY ∧ δZ + ωY ∧ gZ ∈ E p,p (X an ).
Lemma 11.3. Under the hypothesis above and if Z is prime, then we have the
identity
(11.3.1)                  d0 d00 [− log ksk|Z ] = [ωY |Z ] − δD·Z
of δ-currents on Z an .
Proof. This follows immediately from the Poincaré–Lelong equation for s|Z (see
Corollary 7.8). We use here c1 (L|Z , k k) = c1 (L, k k)|Z which follows from
Proposition 9.15.                                                            
Proposition 11.4. Under the hypothesis in 11.2, we have
                          ω(D · Z, gY ∗ gZ ) = ωY ∧ ω(gZ ).
If gZ is a Green current for Z, then gY ∗ gZ is a Green current for D · Z.
Proof. Using Lemma 11.3 and linearity in the prime components of Z, we get
(11.4.1)              d0 d00 [− log ksk ∧ δZ ] = ωY ∧ δZ − δD·Z
and hence 9.14 and Proposition 4.15(iii) give
ω(D · Z, gY ∗ gZ ) = d0 d00 [− log ksk ∧ δZ ] + d0 d00 (ωY ∧ gZ ) + δD·Z = ωY ∧ ω(gZ )
proving the claim.                                                                  

                             −d00 [d0P ηY2 ∧ ηY1 ] − d0 [ηY2 ∧ d00P ηY1 ]
This proves our claim in the third case.
Case 4: k1k1 = 1 and k1k2 = 1.
86                     WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

     In this case ηY1 = − log |f1 | and ηY2 = − log |f2 |. We have to show that
              gY1 ∧ δY2 − gY2 ∧ δY1 = d0 [d00P ηY1 ∧ ηY2 ] + d00 [ηY1 ∧ d0P ηY2 ]
holds. Again, we may assume that f1 and f2 are regular functions on X. By
the previous cases, we may assume that these functions are non-constant. We
use the same notation as above. Here, we choose the very affine open subset
U disjoint from supp(div(f1 )) ∪ supp(div(f2 )). Then ϕ1 , ϕ2 are integral Γ-
affine functions on Trop(U ) inducing − log |f1 |, − log |f2 | on U an . Going the
computation in the second case backwards, we see that
                              Z
(11.5.16) hgY1 ∧ δY2 , αi = −        d00P ϕ1 ∧ d0 ϕ2 ∧ αU + hd00 [ηY1 ∧ d0P ηY2 ], αi.
                                   |Trop(U )|

Note here that d00P ϕ1 ∧ d0P ϕ2 ∧ αU has compact support in Ω. Indeed, it follows
from Corollary 5.6, that d00P log |f1 | ∧ d0P log |f2 | ∧ α is a well-defined δ-form on
X an with compact support in U an (using that the divisors intersect properly)
and hence we get compactness in Ω from Proposition 4.21. Interchanging the
role of Y1 , Y2 and also of d0 , d00 and d0P , d00P in (11.5.16), we get the fourth claim.
This proves the proposition.                                                            
  In the following, we denote the support of a cycle Z (resp. of a Cartier divisor
D) on X by |Z| (resp. |D|).
Corollary 11.6. Let Z be a cycle of X of codimension p and let gZ be any
δ-current in E p−1,p−1 (X). For i = 1, 2, let Li be a line bundle on X with a
δ-metric k ki and non-zero meromorphic section si . Let Di denote the Cartier
divisor on X defined by si . We assume that |D1 | ∩ |Z| and |D2 | ∩ |Z| have both
codimension ≥ 1 in |Z|, and that |D1 | ∩ |D2 | ∩ |Z| has codimension ≥ 2 in |Z|.
Let ηYi := − log ksi ki and let gYi = [ηYi ] be the induced Green current for the
Weil divisor Yi of Di . Then we have
                                                                                   
    gY1 ∗ (gY2 ∗ gZ ) − gY2 ∗ (gY1 ∗ gZ ) ∈ d0 E p,p+1 (X an ) + d00 E p+1,p (X an ) .
Proof. This follows immediately from Proposition 11.5 applied to the analyti-
fications of the prime components of Z.                                    

                          12. Local heights of varieties
  In this section, we study the local height of a proper variety X of dimension
n over K with respect to metrized line bundles endowed with δ-metrics. If the
metrics are formal, then we show that these analytically defined local heights
agree with the ones based on divisorial intersection theory on formal model in
[Gub98]. In particular, they coincide with the local heights used in Arakelov
theory over number fields.
12.1. For i = 0, . . . , n, let Li be a line bundle on X endowed with a δ-metric
k ki and a non-zero meromorphic section si . For the associated Cartier divisor
Di := div(si ), we consider the metrized Cartier divisor D̂i := (Di , k ki ), i.e.
a Cartier divisor Di and a metric k ki on the associated line bundle O(Di ).
Recall from 11.2 that we obtain the Green current gYi := [− log ksi ki ] for the
Weil divisor Yi associated to Di .
      A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                        87

  We assume that the Cartier divisors D0 , . . . , Dn intersect properly. Then we
define the local height of X with respect to D̂0 , . . . , D̂n by
                          λD̂0 ,...,D̂n (X) := gY0 ∗ · · · ∗ gYn (1).

12.2. If Z is a cycle on X of dimension t and D̂0 , . . . , D̂t are δ-metrized Cartier
divisors on X with |D0 |, . . . , |Dt |, |Z| intersecting properly, then 12.1 induces a
local height λD̂0 ,...,D̂t (Z) by linearity in the prime components of Z.
Remark 12.3. The problem with this definition is that it is not functorial as
the pull-back of a Cartier divisor is not always well-defined as a Cartier divisor.
This problem is resolved by using pseudo-divisors instead of Cartier divisors
(see [Ful84, Ch. 2]). We follow [Gub03] and define a δ-metrized pseudo-divisor
as a triple (L, Z, s) where L = (L, k k) is a line bundle on X equipped with
a δ-metric, Z is a closed subset of X, and s is a nowhere vanishing section
of L over X \ Z. Using the same arguments as in [Gub03], we get a local
height λD̂0 ,...,D̂t (Z) for δ-metrized pseudo-divisors which is well-defined under
the weaker condition |D0 | ∩ · · · ∩ |Dt | ∩ |Z| = ∅.
    It is straightforward to show that the local height is linear in Z and mul-
tilinear in D̂0 , . . . , D̂t . It follows from Corollary 11.6 along the arguments in
[Gub03] that the local height is symmetric in D̂0 , . . . , D̂t .
  The next result shows that the induction formula holds for local heights.
Proposition 12.4. Let D̂0 , . . . , D̂n be δ-metrized pseudo-divisors on X with
|D0 | ∩ · · · ∩ |Dn | = ∅. Then the local height λD̂0 ,...,D̂n (X) is equal to
                                Z
        λD̂0 ,...,D̂n−1 (Yn ) −   log ksn kn · c1 (O(D0 )) ∧ · · · ∧ c1 (O(Dn−1 )),
                              X an
where we assume that Dn is a Cartier divisor with associated Weil divisor Yn
and canonical meromorphic section sn of O(Dn ).
Proof. The argument is the same as for [Gub03, Proposition 3.5].                      
Proposition 12.5. Let ϕ : X 0 → X be a morphism of proper varieties over K
and let D̂0 , . . . , D̂n be δ-metrized pseudo-divisors on X with |D0 |∩· · ·∩|Dn | = ∅.
Then the functoriality
                    deg(ϕ)λD̂0 ,...,D̂n (X) = λϕ∗ (D̂0 ),...,ϕ∗ (D̂n ) (X 0 )
holds.
Proof. The proof relies on the induction formula in Proposition 12.4 and the
projection formula for integrals (5.9.1). We refer to [Gub03] for the analogous
arguments in the archimedean case.                                           
  The following result is called the metric change formula.
Proposition 12.6. Suppose that the local height λ(X) with respect to the δ-
metrized pseudo-divisors D̂0 , . . . , D̂n is well-defined. Let λ0 (X) be the local
height of X obtained by replacing the metric k k0 on O(D0 ) by another δ-metric
88                  WALTER GUBLER AND KLAUS KÜNNEMANN

k k00 . Then ρ := log(k k00 /k k0 ) is a piecewise smooth function on X an and we
have                            Z
            λ(X) − λ0 (X) =           ρ · c1 (O(D1 )) ∧ · · · ∧ c1 (O(Dn )).
                               X an

Proof. This follows from linearity and symmetry of the local height in D̂0 and
D̂n and from the induction formula in Proposition 12.4.                      
Remark 12.7. Now suppose that D̂0 , . . . , D̂n are formally metrized pseudo-
divisors on X with |D0 | ∩ · · · ∩ |Dn | = ∅. Then the intersection theory of
divisors on admissible formal K ◦ -models given in [Gub98] induces also a local
height of X (see [Gub03]). It also satisfies an induction formula involving
Chambert-Loir’s measures (see [Gub03, Remark 9.5]). Since the Chambert–
Loir measure agrees with the Monge–Ampère measure (see Theorem 10.5), we
deduce from the induction formula in Proposition 12.4 that the local height
based on intersection theory of divisors agrees with λD̂0 ,...,D̂n (X) from Remark
12.3. This proves in particular Theorem 0.4 stated in the introduction.

                      Appendix A. Convex Geometry
  In this appendix, we gather the notions from convex geometry on a finite
dimensional real vector space W coming with an integral structure. This means
that we consider a free abelian group N of rank r with W = NR := N ⊗Z R. Let
M := Hom(N, Z) be the dual abelian group and let V := Hom(M, R) = MR be
the dual vector space of W . The natural duality between V and W is denoted
by hu, ωi. Let Γ be a fixed subgroup of R. In the applications, it is usually the
value group of a non-archimedean absolute value.
A.1. Let N 0 be another free abelian group of finite rank and let F : NR → NR0
be an affine map. Then F is called integral Γ-affine if F = LF + ω with
ω ∈ N 0 ⊗Z Γ ⊆ NR0 and with the associated linear map LF induced by a
homomorphism N → N 0 .
A.2. A polyhedron ∆ in W is defined as the intersection of finitely many half
spaces {ω ∈ W | hui , ωi ≥ ci } with ui ∈ V and ci ∈ R. If we may choose all
ui ∈ M and all ci ∈ Γ, then we say that ∆ is an integral Γ-affine polyhedron.
A face of ∆ is either ∆ itself or the intersection of ∆ with the boundary of a
closed halfspace containing ∆. We write τ 4 ∆ for a face τ of ∆ and we write
τ ≺ ∆ if additionally τ 6= ∆. The relative interior of ∆ is defined by
                                               [
                            relint(∆) := ∆ \      τ.
                                                  τ ≺∆
Note that every polyhedron is convex. A polytope is a bounded polyhedron.
   A polyhedron ∆ in W generates an affine space A∆ of the same dimension.
Recall that an affine space in W is a translate of a linear subspace and A∆ is the
intersection of all affine spaces in W which contain ∆. We denote the underlying
vector space by L∆ . If ∆ is integral Γ-affine, then the integral structure of A∆
is given by the complete lattice N∆ := N ∩ L∆ in L∆ .
A.3. A polyhedral complex C in W is a finite set of polyhedra such that
     A TROPICAL APPROACH TO NON-ARCHIMEDEAN ARAKELOV GEOMETRY                    89

   (a) ∆ ∈ C ⇒ all closed faces of ∆ are in C ;
   (b) ∆, σ ∈ C ⇒ ∆ ∩ σ is either empty or a closed face of ∆ and σ.
The polyhedral complex C is called integral Γ-affine if every ∆ ∈ C is integral
Γ-affine. The support of C is defined as
                                         [
                                 |C | :=   ∆.
                                         ∆∈C

We say that a polyhedral complex C is complete if |C | = W . A subdivision of
C is a polyhedral complex D with |D| = |C | and with every ∆ ∈ D contained
in a polyhedron of C . This has to be distinguished from a subcomplex of C
which is a polyhedral complex D with D ⊆ C .
A.4. Given a polyhedral complex C in NR , we denote by Cn the subset of n-
dimensional polyhedra in C and by C l = Cr−l the subset of polyhedra in C of
codimension l in NR . We say that a polyhedral complex C is of pure dimension
n (resp. of pure codimension l) if all polyhedra in C which are maximal with
respect to 4 lie in Cn (resp. C l ). Given a polyhedral complex C of pure
dimension n and m ≤ n, we denote by C≤m the polyhedral subcomplex of C of
pure dimension m given by all σ ∈ C with dim σ ≤ m. We set C ≥l = C≤r−l if
r − l ≤ n. Recall here that r is the rank of N .
Definition A.5. (i) A polyhedral set P in NR (of pure dimension n) is a finite
union of polyhedra (of pure dimension n). Equivalently, there exists a polyhe-
dral complex D (of pure dimension n) whose support is P . The polyhedral set
is called integral Γ-affine if the above polyhedra can be chosen integral Γ-affine.
   (ii) Let P be a polyhedral set in NR . A point x ∈ P is called regular if there
exists a polyhedron ∆ ⊆ P such that relint(∆) is an open neighbourhood of x
in P . We denote by relint(P ) the set of regular points of the polyhedral set P .
A.6. A cone σ in W is characterized by R≥0 · σ = σ. A cone which is a
polyhedron is called a polyhedral cone. An integral R-affine polyhedral cone is
simply called a rational polyhedral cone. A polyhedral cone is called strictly
convex if it does not contain a line. The local cone LCω (S) of S ⊆ W at ω ∈ W
is defined by
            LCω (S) := {ω 0 ∈ W | ω + [0, ε)ω 0 ⊆ S for some ε > 0}.
A.7. A polyhedral complex Σ consisting of strictly convex rational polyhedral
cones is called a rational polyhedral fan. The theory of toric varieties (see
[KKMS73, Oda88, Ful93, CLS11]) gives a bijective correspondence Σ 7→ YΣ
between rational polyhedral fans on NR and normal toric varieties over any
field K with open dense torus Spec(K[M ]) (up to equivariant isomorphisms
restricting to the identity on the torus). Then Σ is complete if and only if YΣ
is a proper variety over K.
   A simplicial cone in NR is generated by a part of a basis. A simplicial fan is
a fan formed by simplicial cones. A smooth fan in NR is a rational polyhedral
fan Σ such that every cone σ ∈ Σ is generated by a part of a basis of N . In
particular, a smooth fan is a simplicial fan. A polyhedral fan Σ is smooth if and
only if YΣ is a smooth variety [CLS11, Ch. 1, Thm. 3.12], [Ful93, 2.1 Prop.].
